This Is a Huge Week for the Stock Market: Nvidia Earnings, Fed Minutes, and Anthropic’s IPO

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Nvidia's first earnings report since Jensen Huang resigned, the Fed's meeting minutes, and Anthropic's massive public debut all land this week, and together they could help decide whether the pain in stocks continues or a comeback gets started.

It's been a tough month for the stock market, with the S&P 500 (^GSPC -1.92%) down 2.1% and the Nasdaq Composite down 3.6% since the end of Oct. This week could help decide if that pain continues, or if stocks will make a comeback.

First, Nvidia (NVDA -4.12%) reports earnings tomorrow after the bell. It's the company's first since its now-former CEO Jensen Huang resigned on Nov. 11.

Second, on Wednesday, the Federal Reserve releases minutes from its Oct. 27-28 meeting.

And finally, Anthropic is set for its massive public debut on Thursday, listing under the ticker ANT.

All of this lands amid an already wobbly AI trade that makes up about X% of the entire S&P 500 (^GSPC -1.92%).

Nvidia guided for revenue of about $108 billion, up from $96.2 billion last quarter. Wall Street is looking for slightly more than that, with the consensus sitting at $109 billion -- 91% growth.

But this earnings release is about much more than the numbers. After 33 years at its helm, Jensen Huang resigned last week after multiple reports of extramarital affairs, one of which was with a suspected foreign intelligence asset. The board has launched an investigation into the matter, and CFO Colette Kress will need to answer some uncomfortable questions. The company's dealings with firms that have apparently been contravening U.S. export controls will be under scrutiny. Investors will also want to know in concrete terms how Jensen's departure will affect the business.

The Fed held its benchmark rate steady on Oct. 28, choosing to leave it at 3.75% to 4.00%. However, two officials -- Lisa Cook and Beth Hammack -- dissented in favor of a quarter-point hike. Since the meeting, October's Consumer Price Index (CPI) came in hotter-than-expected while jobs numbers looked better-than-expected.

The minutes, released Wednesday at 2 p.m. ET, will show more of the committee's thinking and whether any of the other officials were open to a hike, despite voting to hold rates.

Beyond the minutes, we get a round of earnings from retailers like Home Depot (HD -0.34%), Target (TGT -0.70%) and Walmart (WMT +0.25%) ahead of the holiday season. I'll be interested to see if there are signs of inflation weighing on consumer spending.

Anthropic plans to sell 825 million shares at $70 to $78, raising as much as $64 billion. That's on top of a $26 billion private placement going to Google, Nvidia, AMD (AMD -5.58%), and others. All told, Anthropic is hoping to walk away with a cool $82 billion, giving it a war chest of $212.7 billion.

Anthropic's growth has been nothing short of incredible -- $33.2 billion in revenue over the first nine months of 2026 versus $2.5 billion a year earlier. But its GAAP net loss was $104 billion (most of this was a paper charge from revaluing convertible notes and other instruments), and it has signed up for something like $900 billion in compute and infrastructure commitments, much of which is non-cancelable no matter what happens. That last part is important. If Anthropic's growth plans don't materialize the way it hopes, the Claude-maker could find itself struggling to make good on those commitments.

Luke Bryan & Jason Aldean Fight $1.4 Million Legal Demand From Nashville Steakhouse Landlord

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The two country stars say they aren't liable for unpaid rent following the closure of E3 Chophouse.

Luke Bryan and Jason Aldean say they aren’t responsible for a hefty real estate judgment against their now-shuttered Nashville steakhouse.

The two country stars filed a motion last month to end the claims brought against them by GBT Realty, the Tennessee property developer that was the landlord for E3 Chophouse. Bryan and Aldean opened the restaurant’s Nashville location in 2019 alongside Adam LaRoche, a retired MLB player who had founded E3 Chophouse in Colorado years earlier.

E3’s Nashville location shut down in February, leading GBT Realty to obtain a $1.4 million judgment against the restaurant for unpaid rent and legal fees. GBT then sued Bryan, Aldean and LaRoche in June, alleging they had signed E3’s lease as guarantors and thus were responsible for footing that bill.

But lawyers for Bryan, Aldean and LaRoche say they’re not on the hook for all that money. Their October court filing, obtained by Billboard, says the restaurant lease was clear that the guarantors’ rent obligations lasted for just five years — and that after that, their only liability to GBT would be for certain construction costs. This amount, they say, comes out to just $59,000.

“The formula provided by the guaranty cap requires simple arithmetic,” wrote the attorneys for Bryan, Aldean and LaRoche. “The court should determine as a matter of law that the guaranty defendants’ liability is limited to the then unamortized portion of the construction allowance.”

Bryan, Aldean and LaRoche are asking a judge to dispose of the case completely with a ruling in their favor. GBT’s reps did not immediately return a request for comment on that request on Monday (Nov. 16). The case is scheduled for a court hearing in January.

Lots of country stars own restaurants and bars in Nashville, from Morgan Wallen to Miranda Lambert to Luke Combs. But with new business ventures come new legal risks, and E3 Chophouse isn’t the only artist-founded establishment to end up in court.

Last month, less than a year after Kane Brown opened Kane Brown’s On Broadway, the restaurant was hit with a $2.4 million lawsuit for allegedly defaulting on its building purchase agreement. Kane Brown’s On Broadway denies the claims and has countersued the seller for breaching confidentiality. The singer himself is not personally involved in that dispute.

Sabre Accelerates Agentic Travel Leadership with First-Mover Travel Experience within Grok Bot and Next-Gen Proactive Travel APIs

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

SOUTHLAKE, Texas, Nov. 16, 2026 /PRNewswire/ -- Sabre (NASDAQ: SABR) will tomorrow preview an agentic travel integration that will bring conversational booking and proactive disruption servicing into frontier AI, beginning with a travel plug-in within Grok Bot.

The breakthrough pairs two industry firsts: the first global travel experience in the Grok Bot ecosystem to enable end-to-end booking and servicing, alongside travel's first proactive, event-driven, agentic-friendly APIs â built to spot flight disruptions and price drops in real time and act automatically without waiting for a prompt.

At Sabre's 2026 Investor Day in New York on Tuesday, November 17, Sabre leadership will highlight both advances and show how agentic infrastructure underpins its multi-year growth plan as the transactional operating system for autonomous travel.

"This is travel's next Internet moment â and our collaboration with SpaceXSI shows where the industry is heading: people asking an AI agent to take care of their end-to-end trip," said Garry Wiseman, President of Product & Engineering, Sabre. "It expands distribution channels for Sabre and our customers by helping them meet travelers wherever they're shopping. And with event-driven APIs behind the scenes, systems won't just wait for questions; they'll spot problems and opportunities early, so an advisor, a bot, or an automated workflow can act fast."

End-to-end agentic solution for all large language models (LLMs): Sabre Mosaic Travel ConnectorWhile basic AI trip search has become widely commoditized, intelligent retailing and post-booking servicing remain travel's complex, high-value next frontier. Sabre Mosaic Travel Connector, which is rolling out in Beta imminently, will enable individual travelers or travel managers to engage directly with an AI assistant to search, book, and service end-to-end itineraries through natural dialogue. Sabre Mosaic Travel Connector will let consumers shop flights and hotels side by side, see what's included and restricted, then book and manage the full trip in one place.

Crucially, the bot will be powered by Sabre's channel-agnostic agentic architecture â connecting conversational interfaces directly with Sabre's Model Context Protocol (MCP) Server and new proactive, event-driven APIs. While debuting in a dedicated environment inside Grok Bot, this underlying modular foundation is designed to scale across all major LLM ecosystems, allowing travel sellers to connect with travelers wherever discovery happens.

Sabre Mosaic Travel Connector joins more than 80 partners in pilot or production with Sabre's MCP services. Having staked out a first-mover position with the launch of its initial agentic APIs and MCP Server in 2025, Sabre maintains nearly a year's head start in live, in-production agentic travel.

Sabre Mosaic's event-driven intelligence is delivered across the full trip lifecycle. Live booking-window insights identify the optimal moment to purchase â down to the ideal number of days in advance, day of the week, or time of day â while Beta air and hotel price-monitoring capabilities track fare and rate thresholds and push alerts the moment prices drop, helping suppliers and agencies re-engage shoppers with timely offers or capture net savings automatically.

Sabre's new proactive, event-driven APIs deliver speed, control, and direct booking integration, working in the background 24/7. This first-to-market technology continuously monitors disruption signals, immediately pinpoints impacted travelers when a flight is delayed or canceled, and pushes real-time alerts into agency systems and connected AI tools without waiting for a prompt.

That intelligence gives travel sellers of all sizes enterprise-grade flexibility in how they respond: a human advisor can step in immediately without manual searches; a conversational interface like Sabre Mosaic Travel Connector can automatically surface policy-compliant options in chat for one-tap traveler approval; or an autonomous agent can execute the fix directly.

Agentic as a Total Addressable Market multiplier

Scaling agentic commerce across new conversational channels represents a significant commercial catalyst for Sabre and its customers. Sabre plans to extend Sabre Mosaic Travel Connector and its channel-agnostic agentic infrastructure across major conversational models and agency workflows throughout 2027 â laying the foundation for proactive, end-to-end agentic travel at scale.

With its technology transformation substantially complete â more than 99% of compute now in the cloud, processing over one billion travel shopping requests daily across roughly 50 petabytes of travel data â Sabre is positioned to convert agentic adoption into profitable growth.

Powering the agentic revolution in travel. Sabre is an AI-native technology leader, backed by one of the world's largest travel data clouds. With AI at its core and operating at unparalleled scale, Sabre transforms insights into innovation, empowering airlines, hoteliers, agencies and other partners to retail, distribute and fulfill travel worldwide. Sabre is built on an open, modular, cloud-native architecture and serves as the backbone for both established leaders and bold, new disruptors, guiding them to the next age of travel retailing through intelligent, connected, and personalized experiences.

The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time remake only just released on November 5, but it's already become the best-selling physical release of the year in the US, a record that'll surely hold against another highly anticipated game releasing in a few days that rhymes with Grand Theft Gelato.

Between newly voiced dialogue, revamped dungeons, fresh puzzles, and more, Hyrule has never felt so alive."

Why Broadcom Stock Got Mashed on Monday

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Investors were reallocating capital to more prominent tech stocks.

Broadcom (AVGO -3.92%) is one of the tech hardware manufacturers spearheading the artificial intelligence (AI) revolution, but you wouldn't know that from its stock's performance on Monday. Investors aggressively traded out of the custom chipmaker's equity, sending it to a nearly 4% loss on a day when the S&P 500 index posted a decline of under 2%. Reallocation into certain peer stocks was a major factor in this, as was an analyst's price target cut.

The shock of the latest Nvidia (NVDA -4.12%) developments hasn't yet worn off. This was affecting investor sentiment not only for tech hardware titles, but for the broader industry as a whole.

At the same time, however, Nvidia is the bellwether AI hardware stock for many. And since it's slated to report its next set of quarterly results tomorrow (Tuesday), many market players remain bullish on its prospects despite the tribulations surrounding co-founder and ex-CEO Jensen Huang. With that, they were selling out of some of their other tech holdings in anticipation of blowout financial results from the company. Ditto for the highly anticipated IPO of star AI model developer Anthropic, whose stock should start trading on Thursday.

These developments were exacerbated by that price target reduction. This was made in a research update by TD Cowen's Joshua Buchalter, who trimmed his fair value assessment on Broadcom to $425 per share from $475. This doesn't make him a bear on the stock, however, as he maintained his buy recommendation.

That, to me, is telling. Price target cuts are normal when stocks and industries are in a dip; this doesn't necessarily mean investors should sell out of either.

In Broadcom's case, it's the go-to company designing application-specific integrated circuits (ASICs; a fancy name for advanced AI chips) supplying the great AI build-out, and that isn't going to change no matter who's in charge of Nvidia, or how powerful a draw Anthropic's IPO proves to be. I'm not wavering from my bullish stance on Broadcom.

Analysis: The Fed's chairman sees an AI revolution coming. Now he has to figure out what it means

📅 2026-11-17  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Artificial intelligence is about to sweep through the economy. The Federal Reserve is bracing for impact.

"We are at a hinge point in the U.S. economy," Fed Chairman Kevin Warsh said at a conference he convened in San Francisco on Monday. But exactly when and how the economy could turn, and what that means for interest rates and inflation, isn't yet clear.

The meeting of Warsh's AI task force saw the new Fed chairman publicly asking the questions Americans will ultimately expect him to answer. He hinted at a few of his private views on whether white-collar workers could soon face massive job losses, but he largely left the speculation to an array of experts he has assembled to advise the Fed.

With the technology racing ahead, Warsh won't be able to put off giving his own answers much longer. AI lab Anthropic plans to price the shares for its initial public offering on Wednesday and could raise as much as $64 billion at a $1.4 trillion valuation. Vast spending from AI firms is prompting the Fed to contemplate raising interest rates in December to slow down the rate of price increases as the tech sector heats up the economy.

Meanwhile Americans are souring on some aspects of the technology. The Nov. 3 midterms saw voters enact an array of state-level measures to slow down construction of the data centers that are essential to powering AI.

Warsh moderated three out of four sessions at Monday's event and was involved in selecting the speakers. Among them was one of the few prominent Democrats who was willing to speak out against that anti-data center trend.

It is "a very troubling sign" that so many anti-data center measures passed in the midterms, Gina Raimondo said. She is a former secretary of commerce under President Joe Biden and a former governor of Rhode Island.

Stopping AI "would be very bad for America" and for the people who voted for the measures, Raimondo said.

Warsh didn't say whether he shares that view, and how it would translate into monetary policy isn't exactly clear.

The event was an unusual one for the Fed. The central bank employs a small army of Ph.D. economists who can rapidly weigh in on anything affecting the U.S. and global economies. But in keeping with his longstanding critiques of the Fed as insular and outdated, Warsh has chosen to draw expertise from outside the institution to help it figure out how to think about what AI will do to productivity and jobs.

Warsh has assembled a task force on that issue, alongside four others devoted to aspects of Fed policymaking.

The public heard Monday from one of the three official members of the task force: venture capitalist Marc Andreessen. Warsh went to Stanford University with Andreessen's wife, and the two have been longtime friends. Another task force member, Stanford economist Chad Jones, is on leave and working at Anthropic and couldn't speak during the company's blackout period ahead of the IPO. The third, Xbox CEO Asha Sharma, didn't attend the event.

Andreessen is hugely optimistic about the promise of AI as a technology, but believes the government won't let Americans experience its full effects. Heavy regulation in areas such as healthcare and education mean technology can't replace people's jobs in those sectors, he said.

"To the extent our economy is a free-market economy, then maybe you could see the, let's say, extremely rapid displacement scenarios people are talking about," Andreessen said. "But we've mostly outlawed the kind of job transition that … both the utopians the doomers talk about."

Warsh returned repeatedly to one scenario for the economy that draws on past experience. The so-called China shock unfolded after that country joined the World Trade Organization in 2001 and moved heavily into low-cost manufacturing. Americans saw prices on clothing and furniture drop rapidly, for instance, but at the cost of severe job losses. One widely cited estimate found the U.S. lost a million manufacturing jobs and 2.4 million jobs overall in the years that followed.

Warsh suggested to one of the panelists that the widespread adoption of AI might produce its own version of the China shock, this time focused on service workers.

"I don't want to hold this too tightly," Warsh said. "But if in some ways over the last generation or generation and a half, the China shock, for lack of a better word, lowered prices of goods in the United States, the way that I heard you talking about the philosophy of this new technology is it might do something for services."

In other words, AI could do, say, legal or administrative work more cheaply than a human. That could lower prices but put many people out of a job.

Warsh asked panelists whether "we should expect disinflation over the medium term, from what AI could do to services."

They largely suggested it was possible, which could mean the Fed might eventually want to lower interest rates over the long term.

But at the end of the day, it is Warsh and his fellow Fed members who will need to have conviction on the answer.

There are still many steps between the task force discussion and any kind of change in Fed policy. First, the three task force members will have to assemble their points of view into a report for Warsh sometime in the next few months. Then the Fed itself will need to debate those results and decide whether or how to use them to shape monetary policy.

But with AI growing bigger every day, the Fed may need to make up its mind sooner rather than later.

Elon Musk Wants Jensen Huang Back at Nvidia. Here’s What’s at Stake for SpaceX

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Elon Musk wants Jensen Huang back at Nvidia and has real money on the line, since SpaceX builds its AI data centers on Nvidia chips and Nvidia owns a multibillion-dollar stake in SpaceX.

Elon Musk wants Jensen Huang back in charge of Nvidia (NVDA -4.12%). On Friday, a day after Huang resigned as CEO, Musk posted on X that a petition to reinstate him was underway and that he was signing it.

Space Exploration Technologies Corp. (SPCX -1.26%) is building its AI data centers exclusively on Nvidia chips, and Nvidia holds a roughly $17 billion stake in SpaceX, so Musk has real skin in the game here.

Huang was pushed out on Nov. 12 after Nvidia's board opened an investigation into reports that he was targeted by a foreign intelligence effort as part of one of now three alleged extramarital affairs. The board named Nvidia CFO Colette Kress as interim CEO as it begins a critical search for a permanent chief executive to replace Huang.

But now, a petition signed by nearly 200,000 people, including CEOs like Musk, Tim Sweeney of Epic Games and Tobias Lütke of Shopify (SHOP +0.49%), is demanding that the board "bring Jensen back while an independent investigation establishes the facts." At the same time, a Financial Times report claims that Huang has been discussing such a return, though just hours ago, CNBC reported a source has told the news outlet that the board isn't considering a return at all.

The financial ties between SpaceX and Nvidia run both ways: Nvidia owns 122.8 million SpaceX shares, worth about $17 billion today, while SpaceX is a major customer -- Musk said in September that its two Colossus data centers in Memphis were running about 780,000 Nvidia chips, with hundreds of thousands more due by the end of the year. Musk said on SpaceX's second-quarter earnings call in August that he expects a "significant allocation" of Vera Rubin GPUs -- Nvidia's newest and most advanced chips -- next year.

Colette Kress is widely expected to rein in the industrywide financing Nvidia has been handing out, especially to neoclouds -- the upstart data center companies that buy Nvidia chips, install them in (often) leased data centers, and rent them out. If that spigot tightens, the consequences for SpaceX may actually be positive.

The company ended Q3 with about $89.9 billion in cash and marketable securities, and has the ability to access traditional financing in a way that some neoclouds cannot, and if Nvidia starts rationing GPUs toward buyers who can actually pay upfront, SpaceX shouldn't have a problem being one of them. That means that the field of competition could narrow, keeping demand for SpaceX's compute high.

That said, SpaceX's AI unit's biggest customer is the AI model builder Anthropic, set to go public later this week. Anthropic depends in part on Neoclouds for the compute capacity it needs to deliver the growth investors expect. Anthropic's own filing warns that if partner financing dries up, contracted capacity could be delayed, repriced, or cut. Though it would likely increase Anthropic's reliance on SpaceX, it wouldn't have the capacity to spare in the short to medium term, and that could put Anthropic in a tough spot financially.

Former Apple chairman Arthur Levinson files to sell $31.8 million in company stock

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Arthur Levinson, who currently serves as Apple’s lead independent director, informed the SEC today of his plans to sell 90,000 shares of Apple stock worth approximately $31.8 million. Here are the details.

If you’ve been following Apple for a while, you likely know the name Arthur Levinson, as he served as the company’s chairman from 2011 until September of this year, when Tim Cook took over as executive chairman as part of the leadership transition that saw John Ternus become CEO.

Back in May, Levinson made headlines when he sold 250,000 Apple shares for approximately $71.2 million. Three weeks later, he sold another 50,000 shares for $15.55 million. On the same day, he also transferred another 65,000 shares as a gift. Still, he retained approximately 3.7 million directly held shares in the company.

Today, Levinson informed the SEC in a Form 144 filing of his plans to sell 90,000 common shares with an aggregate market value of $31,765,885.00, though the disclosure did not specify how many Apple shares he currently holds. The shares are held in The Homeplace Trust, with Levinson acting as trustee and handling the proposed sale on the trust’s behalf.

The filing lists all shares as acquired in cash on February 27, 2001, though it does not disclose how much Levinson originally paid for them. For reference, Apple stock was trading at approximately $0.29 per share on a split-adjusted basis on that date, compared to roughly $353 today.

Importantly, Apple has undergone three stock splits since then, meaning the 90,000 shares listed in the filing don’t necessarily correspond to the number of shares Levinson originally purchased in 2001.

According to the filing, the transaction was expected to take place today through Charles Schwab. However, it is worth noting that Form 144 is only a notice of a proposed sale, meaning the filing does not confirm that the transaction has already been completed.

Johnny Depp is officially back in Hollywood, at least as far as his career is concerned. His 2026 movie release, Ebenezer, just topped the domestic box office, marking his first major studio release in eight years. But while the actor is making his way back onto movie screens (even for lesser pay), he's apparently in no hurry to return to living in the United States. In fact, he's been spending most of his time somewhere that sounds an awful lot like a vacation destination.

The Pirates of the Caribbean star recently spoke with The Wall Street Journal about his childhood, his unlikely introduction to acting and where he calls home these days. I knew Depp owned an island in the Bahamas, but I hadn't realized just how much time he actually spends there. According to the 63-year-old actor:

Today, I live most of the time in the Bahamas. It’s where I’ve found total peace and quiet. Nobody's pointing a camera at me or staring.

I suppose having your own island makes avoiding the paparazzi considerably easier. And considering how much attention Depp's personal life has received over the years, I can understand why he'd want somewhere he can go about his day without somebody pointing a camera at him.

Yet what I found particularly interesting was how the Oscar nominee described the property itself. Depp purchased Little Hall's Pond Cay, a 45-acre island in the Bahamas, in 2004 for a reported $3.6 million. And while I pictured some enormous Caribbean mansion, his description of the place sounds surprisingly modest. He continued:

I bought my 1940s wood house in 2004 and added a yurt and a painting studio. Art continues to be a sanctuary that lets me process the world around me.

A wooden house, a yurt and somewhere to paint. Not exactly what I would have pictured for the man who spent years playing one of Disney's most recognizable characters, but I can appreciate the appeal.

Depp also told the publication that he enjoys reading and playing guitar in his music studio, which sounds about right for somebody who originally moved to Los Angeles hoping to make it as a musician.

Forget Scrooge, Johnny Depp Will Soon Be Back On The Big Screen For What May Be His Scariest Role Ever

His current living situation is especially interesting considering how frequently his family moved when he was growing up. Depp recalled relocating approximately 40 times during his childhood, particularly after his family moved from Kentucky to Florida. The constant changes made it difficult to settle in, and he described himself as an outsider.

Music eventually became his escape. His mother gave him an acoustic guitar when he was 12, and by 1983, Depp had moved to Los Angeles with his band, hoping to make a career out of playing music. Then he met Nicolas Cage.

According to Depp, the two became friends after Cage started attending his band's performances. Eventually, Cage suggested that he try acting and helped connect him with an agent. That led to his film debut in 1984's A Nightmare on Elm Street, followed by the career that made him one of Hollywood's biggest stars.

Of course, his relationship with Hollywood became considerably more complicated in recent years. Depp stepped away from major American studio productions amid legal battles with his ex-wife, Amber Heard, including their highly publicized 2022 defamation trial. He continued working internationally, appearing in the French film Jeanne du Barry and directing Modi: Three Days on the Wing of Madness.

Now, he's back with Ti West's Ebenezer, which opened Nov. 13 and earned an estimated $19 million domestically during its first weekend. But does the box-office win signal a larger Hollywood comeback? Only time will tell, but Depp has another major American release on the horizon: Day Drinker, a thriller co-starring Penélope Cruz, arriving on the 2027 movie schedule. Still, his latest comments suggest returning to American movies doesn't necessarily mean returning to a US address.

The '80s heartthrob has owned his Bahamian retreat for more than two decades, so this isn't some sudden relocation following his Hollywood troubles. But hearing him describe how much time he spends there gives a better idea of what his life looks like these days. After years of playing Jack Sparrow, I suppose it's fitting that Johnny Depp found a little peace on an island of his own.

Sabre Announces Grok Bot Travel Connector and Proactive Agentic APIs

📅 Nov 16, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Sabre Corporation announced on November 16, 2026, that it will preview a Grok Bot travel plug-in at its Investor Day in New York on November 17, 2026, pairing conversational booking and servicing with new APIs that monitor disruptions and prices without waiting for a user prompt.

Sabre describes the plug-in, powered by Sabre Mosaic Travel Connector, as the first global travel experience in the Grok Bot ecosystem to support end-to-end booking and servicing. The connector’s beta rollout is imminent, so the Investor Day preview concerns a forthcoming integration rather than an already-live launch.

The connector is intended for individual travelers and travel managers, who would use natural dialogue to search, book and service complete itineraries. It is designed to display flights and hotels side by side, show what is included and restricted, and let users book and manage the full trip in one place. That extends the AI interaction from discovery into transactions and post-booking service.

Garry Wiseman, Sabre’s president of product and engineering, said the collaboration with SpaceXSI expands distribution channels for Sabre and its customers by helping them reach travelers wherever they shop. He connected that channel strategy to event-driven systems that identify problems and opportunities early so an advisor, bot or automated workflow can respond quickly.

Sabre describes the new proactive APIs as a travel-industry first. They continuously monitor disruption signals, identify travelers affected by delayed or canceled flights, and send real-time alerts to agency systems and connected AI tools without waiting for a prompt.

Responses can remain with a human advisor, who can intervene without manual searches, or move through a conversational interface that presents policy-compliant alternatives for one-tap traveler approval. An autonomous agent can also execute the fix directly. Those options give travel sellers a choice between human handling, traveler-approved automation and autonomous servicing, with direct booking integration supporting the response.

Other Sabre Mosaic functions cover purchase timing and price changes. Live booking-window insights identify when to buy by days in advance, day of the week and time of day. Beta air and hotel price monitoring tracks fare and rate thresholds and pushes alerts when prices fall, helping suppliers and agencies present timely offers or capture savings automatically.

The Grok Bot plug-in is the debut environment for a broader channel-agnostic architecture. Conversational interfaces connect directly to Sabre’s Model Context Protocol (MCP) server, which links AI interfaces to Sabre’s travel services, and to the new event-driven APIs. The modular foundation is designed to extend across major large-language-model ecosystems so travel sellers can reach travelers wherever discovery occurs.

Sabre reports more than 80 partners in pilot or production with its MCP services. Sabre launched its initial agentic APIs and MCP server in 2025, ahead of the connector’s beta rollout.

The company also reports that more than 99% of its compute is in the cloud and that it processes over one billion travel-shopping requests daily across roughly 50 petabytes of travel data.

Sabre plans to extend the connector and its channel-agnostic infrastructure across major conversational models and agency workflows throughout 2027, with agentic infrastructure supporting its multi-year growth plan.

For Sabre, the commercial question is whether its data, booking and servicing infrastructure can become a transaction layer across multiple AI channels. The beta rollout and planned 2027 expansion are the next checkpoints for that strategy.

School Campuses Emerge as the Latest Battleground for Flock Safety Cameras

📅 Nov 16, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

Increasingly, students on college and high school campuses are objecting to the privacy tradeoffs of surveillance equipment.

Over the last year, a backlash against surveillance technologies from companies like Flock Safety has caused agencies in communities, including Los Angeles and Dayton, Ohio, to reevaluate or cancel their contracts for tools such as license plate reader cameras and drones.

Now, some college and high school campuses are taking the same steps, reconsidering whether the value of identifying potential crime suspects or providing first-responder information is worth the privacy trade-offs.

A recent story in The Guardian points to dozens of schools, mostly college campuses, where contracts with Flock have been discontinued or where student protests and activism are pressuring administrations to cut ties. Some of the schools that have walked away from Flock include Cornell University, Emory University, Penn State University and Stanford University, where a coalition called Safe Stanford successfully organized to convince school officials. At Stanford, the university chose to switch from Flock to technology from Genetec, which stores data for only an hour and doesn’t share information with third parties.

Some of the most recent campus protests against using Flock technology have been on the University of Iowa campus, where 2,000 petition signatures were delivered to campus police, and at the University of Kentucky, where student groups chanted, “Flock out! Flock off!”

Flock, which is one of several companies, including Axon Enterprise, Motorola Solutions and Palantir Technologies, that provide surveillance hardware and software to law enforcement, has been heavily criticized for license plate reader failure rates and abuses within law enforcement.

In an email to CNET, the Atlanta-based company declined to offer the number of colleges or schools it works with, though a recent research paper published in Policing & Society put the number at around 150.

Flock stressed in its email to CNET that customers it contracts with own and control their own data.

“Students deserve to feel safe at school, and Flock license plate reader technology has a measurable impact on helping to solve crimes and increase public safety,” the spokesperson said. “Flock LPR cameras capture a point-in-time image of the rear of a vehicle. Flock does not use facial recognition, cannot identify who is inside a vehicle and cannot determine who the vehicle is registered to.”

When government agencies like police departments or county sheriff’s offices employ a company like Flock to provide surveillance services, it typically involves installing cameras in public locations and pointing them at street intersections or on buildings.

College and high school campuses may have different rules for on-campus surveillance and for public schools. They might also be bound by state laws that also apply to government agencies.

“Campuses also raise issues ordinary public streets don’t: students often live there, many are minors, and student-privacy laws and school-search rules vary by state, as do limits on how long plate data can be kept and shared,” said Amy Peikoff, an attorney for the Pacific Legal Foundation.

Peikoff said that most courts have allowed the collection of the types of data Flock Safety collects. However, Peikoff said that problems can arise regarding how long agencies such as police departments or schools can retain the data they collect. In the past, activists have pointed to abuses that have occurred when law enforcement workers inappropriately accessed or shared information collected by Flock, in some cases, using it for stalking.

“Maintaining a vast, queryable database of location histories of individuals about whom there’s no particularized suspicion not only presents an opportunity for abuse, but is in itself, I believe, a Fourth Amendment violation,” Peikoff said.

Shadow welcomes unknown female bald eagle to his nest

📅 Nov 16, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Published Nov 16, 2026 7:41 PM EST

Internet-famous bald eagle Shadow has endured an up-and-down 2026. From suffering the loss of a clutch of eggs to welcoming two chicks, and from losing his longtime partner to finding a new friend, he’s had a tumultuous year.

After the August death of Jackie, the female eagle he raised chicks with since 2018, Shadow began spending time with a 4-year-old female eagle named Kaydee (formerly KD1). Viewers of the 24/7 livestream of Shadow’s nest could easily spot Kaydee because of her double-banded legs—a silver ring on one leg and a lavender one on the other. The pair appeared chummy as they spent more time together, sharing stick-arranging duties, singing, and exchanging beaky kisses. An unexpected twist arrived this week with the appearance of an unknown female bald eagle spotted in the nest with Shadow.

Cameras spotted the unknown female in the nest in recent weeks, but this was the first time the eagle was joined by Shadow. On Monday afternoon, Shadow landed in the nest quickly followed by the unknown female. The unknown female fiddled the nest sticks while vocalizing and Shadow appeared unbothered by her presence. Eagles will typically defend their nest from intruders so Shadow’s willingness to share his space with an eagle other than Kaydee surprised many viewers.

Shadow returned the nest solo later in the afternoon as the sun set over Big Bear Lake.

As this is nature, all we can do is sit back and see what unfolds. Mating season is approaching, so we have to wait and see if Shadow selects a new partner. Female eagles in Big Bear Valley typically lay their first clutch of eggs in January. Tune in to Friends of Big Bear Valley’s (FOBBV) livestreams to follow the southern California eagles anytime.

Kaydee, formerly known as KD1, is a banded female bald eagle that has been spotted in Big Bear Valley following the death of Shadow’s former partner, Jackie. She was born in 2022, about 70 miles away from Big Bear Lake in the Prado Basin between Anaheim and Riverside, California. Coincidentally, her parents are also named Lucky and Ricky like Jackie’s parents, but they are a different eagle pair.

Kaydee was banded as part of the Orange County Water District’s work to track the eagles born in the area. These bands help biologists track and monitor the birds over time. Kaydee currently has a black spot on her head, but it may not be permanent. It’s possible that not all of her white feathers have come in or it could be tree sap or dirt. She also has two small bands on her legs. One is a small metal band, while the other is a lavender band with “KD1” on it.

Kaydee is almost five years old, so she is just about ready to mate. She was recently spotted on FOBBV’s livestream inspecting the nest and has also been seen hanging out near Shadow. Flying around Big Bear is completely normal behavior for young adult eagles at this time of year.

In January, Jackie laid two eggs in the nest, but ravens destroyed them. She later laid two more eggs that hatched in April and named Luna and Sandy. The name Sandy is in honor of FOBBV’s former executive director Sandy Steers who died from cancer in February.

On July 17, Jackie was found injured on the ground near Dana Point Park, California. Cell phone video from a witness confirmed that she had an altercation with two other subadult eagles. She successfully fended off the other eagles by turning over and using her talons, but could not fly far away.

She was transferred to the Ojai Raptor Center for care, where she was diagnosed with severe anemia. Her anemia worsened during the first week of August, the Ojai Raptor Center said that she was in unstable condition on August 9. Her packed cell volume (PCV) fell below percent, before rising to nine percent. Jackie died early in the morning on August 10. The necropsy did not reveal a single cause of death, and instead pointed to several factors including severe anemia, bird flu, blood clots in her wing and one kidney, and liver and lung abnormalities.

A memorial service was held on September 5 in Fawnskin, California, along with a second celebration of life called Wings Over Big Bear on October 3. The event featured representatives from FOBBV, the Yuhaaviatam of San Manuel Nation, and more.

Did a Single Router Ground the Nation’s Flights? Experts Express Doubt

📅 Nov 16, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

Federal Aviation Administration officials say the outage stemmed from an industrial-grade router that began flooding its system.

The humble internet router is at the center of another national firestorm.

Secretary of Transportation Sean Duffy said Sunday that a single router in Arkansas was to blame for the nearly 5-hour-long ground stop on Friday. It was only the second nationwide halt to US air travel since Sept. 11, 2001, leading to the cancellation of 12,462 flights on Friday alone.

FAA investigators zeroed in on a routine network upgrade at a radio facility in El Dorado, Arkansas, where a copper wire line was being upgraded to fiber optic cable by the contractor L3Harris. A new router that came online during the upgrade may have overwhelmed the older equipment, investigators say.

“Either through contractor error or a faulty piece of equipment, that router that was being replaced as we switched from copper to fiber, it started flooding other routers in our air traffic control system,” Duffy said in an interview Sunday.

This sent a barrage of data through the FAA’s internal communication system — about 1,000 connection attempts every 4 to 5 seconds – that air traffic controllers use to help guide pilots through the country’s airspace. When that new router was turned on, agency officials noticed the same issues at a radar site in Amarillo, Texas, which had undergone similar work weeks before, Duffy said. The routers at the Arkansas and Texas facilities led to 17 out of the FAA’s 22 Air Route Traffic Control Centers experiencing network instability, according to the agency.

But some aviation experts say this official explanation strains credulity.

“I don’t think it’s plausible,” Robert W. Mann Jr., the founder of airline analysis and consulting firm R.W. Mann & Company, told CNET. “Given the network architecture, which is fully duplicative and has fallback capabilities on every link and every piece of physical hardware, it’s not credible that one or even two routers would be an issue.”

The impacts of the nationwide ground stop are reverberating on the other side of the weekend. As of 3:30 p.m. EST, 333 flights within, into or out of the US have been canceled Monday, according to flight tracking website FlightAware, and more than 3,800 have been delayed.

The FAA router that grounded tens of thousands of flights looks a lot different from the one that sits in your home. These industrial-grade devices, called switches, are much larger than home Wi-Fi routers and built to withstand harsh conditions. While the agency pointed to the single router in Arkansas as the cause of Friday’s issues, it says it disabled 60 similar units as a precaution.

FAA Administrator Brian Bedford said Saturday that the agency is in the process of replacing around 2,000 Cisco routers with 600 Juniper routers. Could any one of those routine equipment upgrades suddenly overload the FAA’s communications system?

“What surprised me most is the redundancy part for this type of national infrastructure,” Ilia Rabinovich, vice president at the cybersecurity firm Sygnia, told CNET. “This is one of the first things we discuss with clients in terms of preparedness – having backups, having redundancy connectivity, having different connections that you can migrate to different places.”

Mann thought the timing and symptoms pointed to a new $875 million AI-supported air traffic management tool called Strategic Management of Airspace, Routes, and Trajectories, or SMART.

“What they did starting in September at Baltimore-Washington is at a very low level, for about 60 days. It had managed about 180 flights in total over 60 days,” Mann said. “And then suddenly in November, they announced they would implement that system across all 22 continental US air traffic control centers.”

“You went from subscale to superscale based upon 60 days of very, very limited testing, and within two days, you had the meltdown that they should have known was likely to occur,” Mann said.

On Oct. 13, more than 25 lawmakers sent a letter to the FAA asking it to pause the rollout of the tool, citing a lack of transparency and training for air traffic controllers.

“We have heard from several sources that frontline aviation safety workers were notified of the SMART tool rollout mere days in advance or not at all, giving them no time to learn how the system works, how it is incorporated into their workflow, and what oversight and accountability mechanisms maintain aviation safety standards,” the letter says.

The FAA says in an FAQ page on the incident that the SMART system wasn’t responsible for the issue. A representative for the agency didn’t immediately respond to CNET’s request for comment.

The FAA’s acting CTO, Rebecca Guy, said at a press conference Saturday that the FAA identified the routers in Arkansas and Texas and disabled “both of those right away and were able to stabilize our network.” But the Transportation Secretary’s interviews over the weekend didn’t instill a lot of confidence in the FAA network’s resilience.

“We have old switches, old routers. We use 1980s little copper lines. It’s an old system that went down and went down everywhere. It got overloaded,” Duffy said at a Friday night news conference.

The FAA has upgraded over 3,500 of its communication lines to fiber, but more than 5,000 copper lines are still scheduled to be replaced by the end of 2028. It’s eye-opening to see how reliant the FAA still is on copper lines, a technology that the Federal Communications Commission has for years deemed insufficient for average home internet users.

It didn’t take long for officials to point to funding as the ultimate cause of Friday’s groundings. Duffy repeatedly called for more funding over the weekend in order to upgrade the outdated system.

“The government and FAA are looked on as the piñata here because every time you beat on FAA or beat on the government for not doing a good job, money falls out,” Mann said. “It didn’t take, what, 6 hours, before we had a cabinet secretary basically saying if we gave him $50 billion, he would fix all this.”

Duffy had asked for $30 billion for the FAA in September. After Friday’s fiasco, he increased his request by $20 billion.

Unlocking Stable On-Policy RL on TPU with Bitwise-Zero Train–Inference Mismatch

📅 Nov 16, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Reinforcement learning (RL) for large language models is notoriously sensitive to numerical instability: even tiny discrepancies between rollout generation and gradient updates can destabilize training or stall convergence. In LLM RL, the model serves as the policy, π(action | previous tokens), and true on-policy learning requires every action in an update to be generated and evaluated by that exact same policy. While a synchronous loop ensures each rollout batch is scored using the same weights that generated it, a critical numerical requirement remains: the sampler and trainer must evaluate that policy identically.

In practice, production RL stacks run sampling and training through separate, specialized execution paths. The sampler chooses each action token during incremental decoding and records its behavior logprob. The trainer evaluates the same token in batched forward-backward passes to compute its current logprob and gradients. Under the same weights, these differing execution paths can still assign different logprobs to the same token and context. The training schedule is on-policy, but the numerical execution has introduced a hidden off-policy gap that compounds over multi-turn trajectories.

Our zero train–inference mismatch (Zero-TIM) implementation, developed in Tunix and verified at multi-host scale across 64 TPU v5p chips (DP8×TP8), closes this numerical gap. At the same weights, the sampler and trainer assign bitwise-identical logprobs to the same action and token history—bringing state-of-the-art, true on-policy stability to TPU RL training.

A reduction is a tree of floating-point additions. Floating-point addition is not associative, so changing the tree changes where rounding occurs. The effect is easier to expose in bfloat16, which retains less precision than float32.

The following bfloat16 example isolates the effect of reduction order:

The same issue appears in model reductions: matrix products accumulate partial sums, normalization computes statistics, and tensor-parallel shards combine partial outputs. In our original stack, sampler decode and trainer forward used different token-array shapes and forward implementations. JAX specializes computations to their input shapes, and the training forward also runs inside a differentiated program.

Across these paths, reduction order and intermediate precision were not consistently aligned. Floating-point rounding turned those arithmetic differences into different logprob bits. The weights and model equations were identical; the arithmetic was not.

The contract is simple: across sampler decode, serving prefill, and trainer forward, all three paths must produce bitwise-identical logprobs for every sampled action—comparing raw floating-point bytes under the same rollout weights, token history, and sampling settings, with zero rounding or tolerance.

Why three paths instead of two? In a decoupled RL stack, a direct sampler–trainer disagreement shows that a numerical gap exists, but not where it enters. Introducing serving prefill as a middle checkpoint turns alignment into a systematic two-step diagnostic that any RL stack can adopt:

Sampler decode vs. prefill verifies that the serving engine agrees with itself between incremental token generation and full-sequence re-scoring, while prefill vs. trainer verifies that the serving and training engines agree across compilation and differentiation boundaries.

Achieving Zero-TIM does not require merging the sampler and trainer into a single monolithic engine. Instead, we use a generalizable four-part recipe that bridges separate serving and training pipelines across multi-host TPU meshes: locking within-shard kernel accumulation, ordering cross-shard reductions, standardizing the logprob scoring tail, and wrapping serving kernels with custom differentiation rules.

Inside each tensor-parallel rank, batch-invariant Pallas kernels control accumulation order and intermediate precision. Projection kernels traverse fixed contraction blocks in order, keeping the reduction blocking independent of token-row count. Partial products accumulate in float32, with explicit output casts.

The production kernel initializes its scratch accumulator before the tile traversal, then applies this core to each tile:

The next tile continues from the float32 accumulator, not the cast output. This construction covers the attention and MLP projections and the LM head. Dedicated kernels fix RMSNorm reductions and SwiGLU’s intermediate casts. The existing Pallas paged-attention forward is retained.

For row-parallel projections, each tensor-parallel rank produces a partial output. We separate communication from addition: ppermute transports the partials, then every rank adds them in the same source-rank order.

The reduction order is explicit in the program rather than selected by a collective implementation.

Alignment must extend beyond model logits to the probability of the sampled action. Decode, prefill, and trainer apply matching sampling transforms, use the same fixed Pallas log-softmax construction, and select the recorded action token.

Prefill and trainer score the action generated during rollout rather than choosing another action. We also preserve sampled token IDs across environment turns so that every path evaluates the same token history.

The trainer reuses the serving prefill computation. Custom vector–Jacobian products retain the serving kernels for forward evaluation and provide their backward rules. The matmul example shows this separation:

The differentiable replica mirrors the kernel’s accumulation and casts, but is used only to construct the pullback. Forward values still come from the serving Pallas kernel.

Training places that forward inside a differentiated JAX program. We pass --xla_allow_excess_precision=false through XLA_FLAGS to the compiler process, constraining excess intermediate precision in the operations surrounding the Pallas kernels.

Long trajectories are evaluated in fixed-size chunks, carrying the paged KV cache forward. All action logprobs feed the complete train-batch RL loss. The reverse pass propagates gradients through those cache connections and combines parameter-gradient contributions before the optimizer update.

Across the AI systems community, eliminating train–inference mismatch has rapidly become a state-of-the-art requirement for stabilizing large-scale RL convergence. Recent open-source efforts—including vLLM with TorchTitan and RadixArk/LMSys Miles—have demonstrated how batch-invariant kernels and bitwise numerical alignment prevent off-policy drift during policy optimization.

Our work delivers an open-source, modular JAX/XLA and Pallas implementation of this contract for TPU in Tunix. Verified across a multi-host 64 TPU v5p (DP8×TP8) mesh, these kernel and collective patterns provide a reusable blueprint that teams can adopt to achieve bit-exact on-policy RL across distributed JAX/TPU stacks.

To benchmark our TPU kernels, we run a 200-step numerical verification test on the synthetic FrozenLake environment using a public Qwen3-8B checkpoint as a baseline test case using 64 TPU v5p chips with a DP8×TP8 mesh. The recipe uses a GSPO-token loss with leave-one-out advantages. We compare Standard training with trainer-old logprobs and no additional sampler correction, token-level truncated importance sampling (TIS), and Zero-TIM.

The Zero-TIM configuration also uses token-in/token-out (TiTO) handling to preserve sampled token IDs across environment turns. The comparison runs retain their existing tokenization paths.

Zero-TIM records zero mean absolute sampler–trainer logprob difference at every logged step in this window, while its training solve rate increases. Standard and TIS retain nonzero differences: TIS adjusts their contribution to the update without removing the underlying mismatch. Zero-TIM reaches higher observed solve rates in these runs.

We developed Zero-TIM prototype as part of Tunix, Google's open-source, JAX-native library for LLM RL and post-training on Cloud TPU. The kernel patterns and three-way verification contract are modular by design—making them straightforward to adopt across existing multi-host JAX/TPU serving and training pipelines—and we are actively extending support to linear-attention and Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) architectures.

Agents are sessions, not requests: size GKE node pools for AI agent sessions

📅 Nov 16, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

Your company wants to move its coding agents from developer laptops to a shared GKE cluster, for persistence, scale, and governance. Each of those agent sessions stays open all day but works only part of it, and how you run them decides how much of your node bill pays for waiting.

By the end of this article, you will know why an agent session needs a different runtime than a web request, how Agent Substrate and AX run sessions on GKE, and how to identify cost optimization opportunities in your GKE node pools. This article assumes you know Kubernetes Pods and node pools. If not, check out the GKE overview.

A web request lasts milliseconds. The server answers it, keeps nothing, and serves the next one, so Kubernetes scales a web app by its traffic: more requests, more Pods.

An agent session works another way. A coding agent's workspace can stay open for the developer's whole workday: it keeps its memory and its files between prompts, and it works only after a prompt arrives.

How much of that day does the agent really work? Much less than it waits. In a study of GitHub Copilot agent traces, Banruo Liu and colleagues found that in a typical session with several prompts, the agent waits for the user about 80% of the time. A turn, one prompt and the agent's whole response to it, takes 396.3 seconds of agent work on average.

Put those numbers on one developer's day. With 2 prompts an hour over an 8 hour workday, which is our assumption, the agent works 22% of the day and waits for the other 78%. If each session holds a machine, you pay for that 78%.

Teams usually pick one of two answers, and both work well for web apps.

The first is to give each session its own Pod. The session keeps its state, but its Pod holds 1 vCPU and 1 GiB all day. For example, a c3-standard-4 node fits 3 such Pods after GKE's reservations, so in our cost model 100 coding agents need 34 nodes and about USD 1,288 a month, for agents that work 22% of the time.

The second is to scale the Pod to zero between prompts. The node is free, and files on a persistent volume survive, but the process and its memory do not, so the next prompt starts a new agent process.

Based on that, you want a third option: keep the session, and free the machine while the session waits.

Agent Substrate and AX are built for that: the developer's session stays open, and the sandbox that serves it gives its machine back while the session waits. Together they cover the parts of an agentic platform that are the same for every team: the control plane, the runtime, and the sandbox. Agent Substrate answers where and how an agent runs. AX answers what the agent is.

Following the CNCF donation of gVisor, Agent Substrate, and OpenRL in November 2026, you have the flexibility to run this open source, agentic platform across any environment. Agent Substrate 1.0 is generally available on GKE.

One distinction helps from here on. The session is the multi turn interaction between the developer and the agent, with its conversation history. The compute that serves it is an isolated sandbox: an actor in Agent Substrate, or a Task in AX. Parking acts on the sandbox, and the session stays open.

Agent Substrate answers where and how an agent runs. It separates the stateful agent from the compute it runs on. An actor is the sandbox for one agent, and it holds the agent's running processes, files, and identity. A worker is a reusable sandbox drawn from a warm pool of Kubernetes Pods, and one worker hosts several actors.

An agent runs code that a model wrote, so anything in its environment can leak through a prompt injection or a bad command, API keys included. Gateways control the actor's inbound and outbound traffic and add credentials on the way out, based on the actor's identity, so the agent never holds the keys.

SuspendActor snapshots an idle actor to object storage and frees its worker, and the next request restores the actor on any warm worker. Using this approach, we can achieve higher density by multiplexing more agents to run on the same amount of compute.

AX answers what the agent is and what it knows. You declare a Task, which says what runs (image, command, and limits), and a Workspace, which says what the agent knows (repositories and files). AX runs each Task as an actor. ax suspend parks the task and keeps its /workspace. Today AX asks for a volume snapshot, so the files come back on resume and the agent's processes start fresh (runner guide).

AX passes suspend, resume, and delete to Agent Substrate. The AX roadmap adds budgets, policies, and gateways, which limit what an agent may do, and a record of what each task did.

Two details decide the bill before you size anything.

First, you decide when a sandbox parks. Agent Substrate does not suspend an idle actor by itself (#424), so your agent harness, or the service in front of it, calls the suspend API after an idle timeout. Until then, an idle actor keeps its worker. When idle actors hold every worker, a request for another actor waits up to 5 seconds by default, then fails with HTTP 503 (Agent Substrate docs).

Second, you decide how much CPU each sandbox is promised. An actor is sized by its CPU and memory limits, and each worker books those limits against the capacity you declare for its pool (API guide). Our cost model sets that capacity to what the node really has, so every actor keeps its full limit. You can declare more capacity than the node has, and then you overcommit CPU at the pool instead of in each Pod.

Our cost model prices parking for the same 100 coding agents, at Google Cloud list prices in us-central1.

With a 2 minute idle timeout, each session holds a worker 29% of its open time. A warm pool sized for bursts (the 99th percentile of sessions holding a worker at once, if sessions act independently, plus 15% headroom) needs 15 nodes. Add one node that runs around the clock for the Agent Substrate control plane and its PostgreSQL database, and a job that scales the pool to zero after hours:

Parking saves 40% of the node bill. The pool is full about once in 3,500 turns, so a prompt rarely meets that 503. In an hour at 2.5 prompts instead of 2, the same 15 nodes are full for 3% of turns, so size the pool for your busiest hour.

Keep in mind that parking pays most when your agents wait the most. At 2 prompts an hour, the saving is 40%. At 4 prompts an hour, the agent works 44% of the hour, the pool grows to 27 nodes, and the saving is 4%. Around 4.5 prompts an hour, one Pod per session becomes the cheaper option, so measure your prompt rate first.

Also, the pool size is yours to set. The autoscaling option in the GKE install guide follows worker Pods, not suspended actors, so scale the pool to zero after hours with a scheduled job: a pool that runs all 730 hours of the month costs about twice the one Pod per session bill.

Finally, the fleet size matters. The Agent Substrate control plane costs the same for 10 sessions or 1,000, so parking pays from about 37 coding sessions, or 13 for an assumed chat assistant that works about a minute an hour.

Overcommit changes the comparison. If your Pods request less CPU than their limit, Kubernetes places them by their requests. A Pod that requests 0.25 vCPU with a 1 vCPU limit packs 12 to a node, so the same 100 coding agents fit on 9 nodes, fewer than our parked pool. Agent Substrate can overcommit too, through the capacity you declare on the worker pool, and our cost model does not price that case.

Overcommit has the same cost on both sides: agents share CPU and slow down when their neighbors are busy, during builds and tests. Compare like with like before you decide to park: Pods and actors with the same CPU guarantee.

Back to the shared cluster. Agents are sessions, not requests: size the warm pool for the sessions that hold a worker, not the sessions that are open.

We would park long lived sessions that wait most of the hour, and keep one Pod per session when developers prompt more than about 4 times an hour. Size the warm pool for working time plus a short idle timeout at your busiest hour, and compare with your current Pods at the same CPU guarantee before you park.

Thanks for reading! If you run agent sessions on Kubernetes today, how many prompts an hour do your users send? The prompt rate is the input we are least sure of. You can reach me on LinkedIn or X with your numbers, questions, and corrections.

UK Conservative leader Kemi Badenoch is more worried "about the amount of time" people will spend on GTA 6 than the game's violent content

Leader of the UK Conservative Party (and former Mortal Kombat player) Kemi Badenoch is more worried about the amount of time people will spend playing GTA 6 rather than the game itself.

Every new Grand Theft Auto game invites some amount of controversy over its in-game violence or depictions of sex and drugs - from San Andreas' raunchy Hot Coffee mini-game to GTA 5's graphic torture scene. Ahead of GTA 6's release this week, the Tory leader downplayed those fears.

While Badenoch's stance might be surprising for the normally right-wing UK political party, she did still worry over children who might play GTA 6 for too long. "I haven't played Grand Theft Auto, I know people are worried about the game content. They are two different things," Badenoch added.

Reminder Backpressure: Scheduling Email, SMS, and Push Through Rate-Limited Workers

📅 Nov 16, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

A SaaS user reminder scheduling API should store mutable property-management intent in a due table, then feed email, SMS, and push jobs into a rate-limited queue; cron alone can wake work, but it cannot prove that a tenant received anything.

Short answer: for user reminders sent through a rate-limited pool, keep future intent in a due table, claim it with a short lease, and enqueue only ready work. Let workers deliver email, SMS, or push with an idempotency key. This gives the best delivery evidence without pretending that cron or a queue can prove receipt.

The recommendation is deliberately boring. Use the database for mutable calendar intent and a queue for backpressure. A lease makes crashed claims recoverable; a stable delivery key makes repeats harmless at the application boundary. Then record every attempt separately from the reminder itself.

Start by naming the guarantees. A reminder API accepts intent. A scheduler decides when that intent becomes eligible. A queue moves eligible work. A channel adapter attempts delivery. Those are four different promises, and folding them into one scheduled flag makes operations look cleaner than they are.

For a property manager, a lease-expiry notice may be edited after a tenant renews, while a maintenance-window alert may fan out to hundreds of residents. The former needs cheap cancellation and a clear current state. The latter needs controlled throughput. A future row handles edits well; a ready queue absorbs the burst without asking the database sweeper to wait behind slow channel calls.

Use at-least-once processing as the working assumption. A worker can finish the external call and lose its acknowledgement, so the same job may return. Exactly-once delivery is not a useful API promise here — the scheduler does not control the channel provider or the recipient's device. What it can control is a stable key such as propertyId:reminderId:channel:revision, plus an atomic record showing whether that revision has already been accepted for delivery.

This distinction is small on a diagram and huge at 09:00, when every building's daily reminder becomes due. Don't make the scheduler call providers directly. It should claim, enqueue, and get out.

The state machine needs more precision than pending and sent. I use names such as scheduled, claimed, ready, attempting, accepted, retryable, and terminal. Accepted means the channel adapter accepted the request; it does not mean a human read it. Keep provider receipts or later delivery events as separate evidence.

A claim should carry a lease token and expiry. The dispatcher selects due rows, changes them from scheduled to claimed in the same database transaction, and commits before publishing ready jobs. After publication it records ready. If it stops between commit and publish, a reconciler can reclaim expired leases. If it publishes and stops before recording ready, the queue may contain a duplicate. The delivery key handles that branch. This is why the hybrid has two recovery mechanisms: leases repair missing queue messages, while idempotency absorbs repeated ones. Neither substitutes for the other. Dead-letter queues become useful after a message crosses the retry threshold: they isolate repeatedly failing work for inspection or redrive rather than cycling it forever, but they are not an archive and they don't explain the failure by themselves. Store structured attempt data — reminder ID, revision, channel, attempt number, eligibility time, claim token, adapter outcome, and correlation ID — while keeping message content and tenant contact data out of logs.

One sharp edge: preserve ordering only where the product requires it. A cancellation or corrected notice must not be overtaken by an older revision. Unrelated tenants do not need a global order, which would turn one slow job into a building-wide traffic jam. Compare revisions at delivery time and discard superseded work as a successful no-op.

The clock needs an explicit policy too. Store an absolute instant for dispatch and retain the user's time zone plus the rule that produced it. Recalculate future rows when the user edits that rule. Cron is good at recurring activation, but its traditional schedule describes recurring wall-clock times; it is not a per-recipient delivery ledger.

Keep the scheduling API small. Config bloat usually starts when transport settings, retry policy, tenant time zones, and provider credentials all leak into one request. The caller should submit reminder intent. Operations policy belongs behind the boundary.

The values 200 and 30_000 are sample controls, not universal defaults. Benchmark them against the database, queue, and strictest channel allowance. I care about four measurements: claim latency, oldest-ready-job age, attempts per accepted delivery, and terminal outcomes by channel. Throughput alone can look healthy while one tenant's reminder sits behind a hot property.

Workers should take capacity from a channel-specific limiter before calling the adapter. A shared pool with separate email, SMS, and push budgets can use spare concurrency without letting a burst on one channel consume every slot. On a retryable outcome, add jittered delay and preserve the same delivery key. On a terminal outcome, record it once and stop. The exact classification depends on the adapter contract; I'm not sure a generic library can choose it correctly without application-specific policy. Tests should pin that contract.

Test the ugly boundaries with a fake clock and deterministic adapters: a crash after claim, a crash after publish, two dispatchers racing for the same row, a revision change while a job waits, exhausted channel capacity, and redrive from the dead-letter queue. Run a load test with a deliberately tiny allowance — for example, five concurrent sends — and check oldest-job age, not just average throughput. Fast averages hide starvation.

Stick with a cron sweep when the reminder set is small, a few minutes of timing drift is acceptable, and the team already operates a transactional database. It has the shortest time-to-first-call and the least infrastructure. The catch is that scanning, claiming, retrying, and deduplicating are still your job; cron only starts the sweep.

Choose delayed messages as the source of truth when reminders are short-lived, rarely edited, and the broker's supported delay and retention behavior match the product requirement. This can remove the dispatcher and its due-row index. It is not suitable when users frequently reschedule far-future reminders or when product support needs a simple query for every pending notice. Your mileage may vary because broker semantics differ; verify maximum delay, retention, redrive, ordering, and cancellation before committing.

The hybrid is also a poor fit for a tiny service with one daily digest and no burst problem. Two stages mean two dashboards, a reconciler, and more deployment surface. Start with the cron sweep there. Move to the hybrid when worker saturation, edit volume, or recovery evidence becomes a measured problem.

No option makes multi-channel delivery exactly once. Pick the smallest design that keeps intent recoverable, constrains the worker pool, and leaves evidence an operator can trust.
